- **Texture stages.** `TexStage(n)` selects the stage that `Enable(Texture2D)`, `TexEnv`, `TexParameter`, `TexStageCoord`, `TexStageMatrix` and `TexStageCombine` apply to. `TexEnv(int)` sets the GL texture env mode (replace, modulate, decal, blend, combine); `TexEnv(float*)` sets the constant colour (Direct3D's texture factor). `SetCombiner(combiner, stage)` takes a 10-byte `cGDCombiner`: RGB op, three RGB args, alpha op, three alpha args, RGB and alpha scale; an arg's low nibble is the source (texture, previous, constant, primary colour), the high nibble the operand (colour, one minus colour, alpha, one minus alpha); RGB op 0xFF leaves the stage alone. `TexStageCoord(n)`: the low 3 bits pick the coordinate set, 0x10/0x20/0x30 generate coordinates from the camera-space position, normal or reflection vector.
- **State.** `Enable`/`Disable`: 0 alpha test, 1 depth test, 2 stencil test, 3 culling (clockwise faces), 4 blending, 5 texturing on the current stage, 6 fog, 7 nothing. Depth, alpha and stencil functions, stencil ops, blend factors and shade model are GL's lists in GL's order. `PolygonOffset(n)` is a depth bias: the DirectX driver (0x00883710) sets Direct3D 7's `D3DRENDERSTATE_ZBIAS` (0-16, higher draws in front) to `n`. The game calls it about once per object, always with 0 in the test city.
- **Fog.** The DirectX driver supports only vertex fog, and only when the device reports it: `Enable(6)` sets `D3DRENDERSTATE_FOGENABLE`, `Fog(mode, value)` sets `D3DRENDERSTATE_FOGVERTEXMODE` (exp, exp2, linear), `Fog(param, float*)` sets the colour, density, start and end (0x00883100, 0x00883180). Neither driver received a fog call in the test city, with `RenderCloudsAndFog` on or off: the clouds are drawn as textured sprites and terrain passes, not with fog.
- **Errors.** Methods set an error code that `GetError` returns and clears (DirectX driver: 1 bad enum, 2 bad value, 3 unsupported, 6 failed mode set).
- **Not implemented by either driver:** the six blits (`BitBlt`, `StretchBlt` and their alpha variants) only set an error. `GetBoolean` and `GetFloat` are empty in the DirectX driver; `GetInteger` answers only the unpack row length.
- **Buffer regions** (`cIGZGBufferRegionExtension`): `NewBufferRegion(surface)`, with 0 the back buffer (the render target) and 1 and 2 depth, returns a handle (from 1) to a surface-sized copy (until 2026-10-09 our driver took 0 for the front buffer and 1 for the back buffer, so the city view saved and restored the wrong pictures; nothing showed it until photo mode hid every interface window); `ReadBufferRegion(h, rx, ry, w, h, x, y)` copies (x, y, w, h) of the surface to (rx, ry) in the region, and `DrawBufferRegion(h, rx, ry, w, h, x, y)` copies (rx, ry, w, h) of the region back to (x, y) of the same surface: the region's position comes first (the DirectX driver's blits, 0x008853B0 and 0x008855F0). Top-left origin. The game uses them to restore parts of the frame instead of redrawing them (about 16,600 draws per 120 s), and to scroll the city view: it moves the saved picture and draws only the strip that comes into view. Until 2026-10-06 our driver had the two positions swapped, which made no difference while they were equal (all 20 shots matched) but scrolled the picture the wrong way, leaving streaks where the new strip should be.
- **Snapshots** (`cIGZGSnapshotExtension`): `CopyColorBuffer(x, y, w, h, buffer)` copies part of the front buffer into a 32-bit `cIGZBuffer`, creating one (class 0xC470D325) if `buffer` is null, and returns it.
- **Dynamic vertex buffers** (0x09CD86F9, a separate object; DirectX driver vtable 0x00AC4F10 over Direct3D 7 vertex buffers): slot 3 `GetBuffer(vertexFormat)` returns a handle (always 0 in the DirectX driver), 4 the buffer's format, 5 its capacity in vertices (64 KB worth: 2,048 of the 32-byte format 0xB), 6 `Lock(buffer, count)` reserves `count` vertices after the previous batch, starting over at 0 when they do not fit, starts a new batch and returns a pointer to write them to, 7 `Append(buffer, count)` extends the locked batch, 8 `Unlock`, 9 `Draw(buffer, primitive)` and 10 `DrawIndexed(buffer, primitive, count, uint16 indices)` draw the batch with the current state (indices relative to its first vertex), 11 resets every buffer. The terrain (0x007545C0) and the scene renderer (0x00593E10) draw through it, about 180,000 queries per 90 s shots run; the stock OpenGL driver does not offer it, which is one reason it draws no terrain.
- **Texture transforms.** `TexStageMatrix(m, inputs, outputs, flags)`: the terrain shadow projection uses camera-space coordinates (`TexStageCoord(0x10)`) with (4 inputs, 2 outputs, flag 1). The DirectX driver negates the matrix's third row in that case (and for 3 inputs without flag 1); in our driver the matrix as given matches the stock output better, so Direct3D 7's camera space evidently has the opposite z. The OpenGL driver instead generates only s and t from the eye position.

## The software rasterizer

The GZ framework's 2D drawing, 0x0082B780 to 0x00864B5F (916 functions; [uncharted-code.md](uncharted-code.md), "Block 1"). Mapped with Ghidra on 2026-10-26; addresses in [raster.toml](../data/addresses/raster.toml). Names are ours.

- **Who uses it.** A drawing context, vtable 0x00AC16C8 (200 bytes, constructor 0x0082C9A0, interface 0x2AE63219, the one [region_view_3.cpp](../src/core/region_view_3.cpp) asks for). The factory's slot 5 (0x0082B980, vtable 0x00AC1668, interface 0x4B2EA3B3) makes a 0x4C-byte buffer with a drawing context (0x0082B780, vtable 0x00AC1550) together with a drawing context for it: the buffer panels draw into ([ui-scaling.md](ui-scaling.md), "Panels get tiles of their own"). The classes are registered with the framework's DLL director by 0x00825B60 (five through `cRZCOMDllDirector::AddCls`). So this is the canvas of the interface's CPU pictures, not of the city view.
- **The drawing context.** Keeps the target buffer (+0x24), a stack of 0x18-byte states (+0x7C/+0x80: the origin's x and y as floats at +0x2C, the clip at +0x34) and one of 0x10-byte draw settings (+0x88/+0x8C), four colours (+0x04, and the target's own pixel values of them at +0x14, through cIGZBuffer slot 0x78), a pen width (+0x4C, at least 1 as an integer at +0x50), and the rasterizer (+0x28). SetTarget (slot 17, 0x0082C830) makes the rasterizer through the framework (class 0xEB2EA3CB, interface 0x4B2EA3B3) and hands it the target, the clip, the colours and the settings. Its draws turn integer rectangles and points into floats and go to slot 44 (0x0082EA60, adds the origin) or 45 (0x0082FC30, nine primitive kinds). Blt (slot 38, 0x0082C530) and slot 39 (0x0082C620) set a source, run it and reset.
- **The rasterizer** (vtable 0x00AC17D0, 23 slots, 0xD4 bytes; made by 0x0082B910, constructor 0x00830690, two places Ghidra's analysis had not reached) holds the target locked and one span renderer for its pixel format (made by Attach, 0x00830710; vtables 0x00AC182C, 0x00AC1844, 0x00AC185C, 0x00AC1874, 0x00AC188C, six slots each: destructor, fill, gradient, blend, blended gradient, copy). Two more classes share six of its slots (vtables 0x00ADB500 and 0x00AEFDE0; the "companion" 0x00A64110 to 0x00A66741) and have their own copies of the rest.
- **What runs.** Only the 32-bit renderer (0x00AC1874), and of it four routines: FillSpans (slot 1, 0x00854E10), CopySpans (slot 5, 0x008551D0), which picks a table from 0x00AC23D8 by the pattern's word 4 and byte 0x1C and calls entry `op` of it, and the two entries of table 0x00AC2228 that were picked: the plain copy (entry 0, 0x0085B030) and the tiled copy (entry 8, 0x0085B550). Spans are pairs of words, (y << 16 | x) of the first pixel and of the one past the last. The ~770 span routines are the other formats' (the 16-bit ones the survey decompiled) and the blends, colour keys and scalings, none of which ran.

**Ours** ([raster_spans.cpp](../src/core/raster_spans.cpp), `renderer.raster.spans`, off by default, compare mode offered): the four routines, a pixel at a time, forward, addresses in 32 bits as the game's; the tiled copy's column and row are unsigned remainders of the shifted position, the column wrapping to 0 within a span. CopySpans reads the same table, so it reaches the game's copies or ours. None of the four keeps ECX or EDX, so the detours need no thunks.

**The drawing context's methods** ([raster_context.cpp](../src/core/raster_context.cpp), `renderer.raster.context`, off by default, no compare mode): 36 of its 48 slots, every one that ran except QueryInterface (0), SetTarget (17) and the two draws (44, 45), which have groups of their own below. Names are ours and in [raster.toml](../data/addresses/raster.toml). What they do:

- AddRef and Release (count at +0xC4; at 0 the game's destructor 0x0082CA40 and operator delete). The AddRef is shared with two other classes' vtables (identical code).
- Two stacks: PushTransform (slot 3) and PopTransform (9) keep the origin and clip (0x18 bytes at +0x2C) on the vector at +0x7C, grown by the game's push 0x00627100; PushSettings (10) and PopSettings (11) keep the settings (0x10 bytes at +0x44) on the one at +0x88, grown by 0x00638C60. PopSettings puts them back through the context's own setters.
- The clip: ResetClip (4, the whole target, the rasterizer not told), ClipTo (5, cut to a rectangle moved by the origin, truncated) and ClipToAbsolute (6); the cut is 0x0079DD60's (all four comparisons strict; no overlap gives an empty rectangle). The clip the rasterizer gets (its slot 6) is also cut to the source rectangle (+0x64) while a whole source is set (+0x74 set, +0x75 clear).
- The origin as floats (SetOrigin 8 from an integer point, GetOrigin 14 truncated), the stack depth (15), HasTarget (16).
- Settings: SetColour (20: the colour, its pixel value from the target's slot 30, all four to the rasterizer's slot 7) and slot 21 for colour 0; a mode (22, +0x48) and two flags (23 and 24, bytes +0x45 and +0x44, the whole argument handed on), each told to the rasterizer only when it changes; the pen width (25: unchanged leaves it, NaN never is; 0 unless above 0; plus 0.5, truncated, at least 1, at +0x50); ResetSettings (19) puts all of them back.
- The source: SetSource (29: the position plus the origin, worked out in doubles as the x87 does at the game's 53-bit precision, to the rasterizer's slot 13, which fills the source rectangle and answers whether it took it), the whole of it (30) or a part (31), ClearSource (28).
- Rectangles as floats to the draws: FillClip (32, slot 45), FrameRect (34, each side plus 0.5, slot 44), FillRect (35, slot 44). Blt (38: the source at the target's corner less the source's, scaled by their sizes divided in doubles) and TiledBlt (39: at the corner plus an offset, scale 1, the part set from the source rectangle) skip empty rectangles.
- A batch count (41 to 43: Begin calls the rasterizer's 16 and 17, End its 18 when the count returns to 0) and three plain forwards (40, 46, 47). Slot 46 (GetSurface) is a tail jump in the game to the rasterizer's slot 19, which takes two arguments (ret 8), so ours passes on two; the first version passed none and only worked because the compiler made it a tail jump too.

Every one of ours answers what the game's leaves in EAX (the last call's answer, the argument, a pointer): the panel code at 0x0082A6AD reads the origin through GetOrigin's answer, which is the point it passed. The first version answered nothing there and crashed at startup. The one answer not reproduced is an unchanged pen width's, the x87 status word.

**The draws** (same file, `renderer.raster.draws`, off by default, no compare mode): Draw (slot 44, 0x0082EA60) adds the origin to the points into the float vector at +0x94 and calls slot 45; DrawShape (45, 0x0082FC30) swaps the three words at +0xA0 with those at +0xAC (left as the game does it; nothing that ran uses them) and picks one of nine shapes by kind. Two ever ran: kind 3, the filled rectangles of FillRect and FillClip (Fill 0x0082FAA0), and kind 1, FrameRect's lines (Outline 0x0082FBE0). Each asks the rasterizer (slot 21) how to draw; it answered 2, spans of the context's own, collected in the vector at +0xB8 and handed to the rasterizer's slot 22. Ours: FillRects (0x0082F240: each pair of points a rectangle, its sides ceil(v - 0.5) truncated, cut to the clip, one span a row), Lines (0x0082E8A0: ends in 24.8 fixed point, floor(v * 256 + 0.5)) and Line (0x0082D040). Line is written after the game's registers: bit 0 of each end set, u the major axis and v the minor (swapped when steep), the ends swapped so u runs toward the clip's far side, an error term in units of twice the axes' lengths, the clip entered and left along v with divisions, the first and last pixel kept or dropped by the sum of the ends' distances from their pixel centres, one one-pixel span a step. Every Line call hands all the spans gathered so far to the rasterizer, and Lines hands them all again at the end, so a frame's earlier lines are sent more than once. The other seven shapes and the rasterizer's answer 1 stay the game's, called by address.

**SetTarget** (same file, `renderer.raster.target`, off by default, no compare mode): slot 17, 0x0082C830. The target already held changes nothing and leaves EAX as the caller had it, so that test is a few lines of assembly in front of the body. Otherwise the rasterizer is detached (its slot 4) and released, the new target held and the old released; with no new target that is all, and the answer is the old target's release. With one, its rectangle (cIGZBuffer slot 12) becomes the clip and the origin 0, the framework (RZGetFramework, slot 5) makes the factory (class 0xEB2EA3CB, interface 0x4B2EA3B3), whose slot 4 makes the rasterizer into +0x28 for the target; the rasterizer is then told the target (3), the clip (6), the colours (7), the mode and flags (8 to 10), the pen (11), no source (13) and slot 12, and the whole area (5, +0x54, whose answer is SetTarget's); the source and batch flags are cleared.

**The rasterizer's methods** ([raster_rasterizer.cpp](../src/core/raster_rasterizer.cpp), `renderer.raster.rasterizer`, off by default, no compare mode): all 19 that ran besides the shared ones. Names are ours and in [raster.toml](../data/addresses/raster.toml).

- Make (0x0082B910, stdcall) asks the target for the buffer interface (0x20732180) only to test it, makes 0xD4 bytes and runs the constructor (0x00830690: the vtable, count, target, source, renderer and four flags zeroed), AddRef. Release (slot 2) at 0 deletes the renderer and releases the source and the target, which it does not unlock.
- Attach (3): Detach, the target held and locked (cIGZBuffer slot 6 with 0x8040), a span renderer for its format (2, 4 to 9; 8 and 9 are the 32-bit one that runs; any other releases the target, still locked, and refuses) and its surface at +0x78, six words as the span renderers take them: bits, stride, width, height, format, bytes a pixel. Detach (4) deletes the renderer and unlocks and releases the target.
- The setters: the clip (6, +0x14), colours and their pixel values (7, +0xB4 and +0xC4), the mode (8: +0x41 whether it is above 0, which turns the fills and copies into blends), the context's two flags (9 and 10, +0x42 and +0x43), a gradient (12: three rows of four channels, base and steps along x and y, from 0 to 1 times 255 in 16.16; the context only ever clears it), the source (13), the whole source or a part (14, 15). UnlockTarget and LockTarget (17, 18) are the context's batches: drawing between them would write into an unlocked target. GetSurface (19) copies the surface out.
- SetSource: the source held at +0x24 and its rectangle (cIGZBuffer slot 11); scales in 16.16 (scale * 65536.0) and their inverses (65536.0 / scale), each rounded to a float and then to the nearest integer as FISTP does (ties to even). At scale 1 the position is rounded and the rectangle moved by it; otherwise the position is kept in the source's 16.16 and the rectangle's sides come from the position less 0.5 and the scaled size, rounded by adding 1.5 * 2^23 as a float (one less when the value is already a whole number).
- HowToDraw (21) answers 2, the context's own spans, unless +0x43 is set; then kinds 6 to 8 go to a polygon routine (0x008308D0) that never ran and stays the game's, and the rest answer 1.
- DrawSpans (22): without a source, the renderer's fill (slot 1) with colour 0's pixel value, or its gradient (2), blend (3) or blended gradient (4). With one, the source locked (0x800), its surface read, its format turned into the copy tables' index, its colour key masked to the format's bits, and the renderer's CopySpans (5) with an eight-word pattern and an op (blend, the +0x42 flag, 8 for a part, 4 for scaled): a part moves the bits to its corner and gives the tiled copy its shifts (0x7FFFFFFF less its remainder less the position, modulo the part's size), scaled gives the halved inverse less the position and the inverses, and plain moves the bits back by the position.

Answers: the game's leave EAX as the caller had it in five places (the two flag setters, UseWholeSource, the lock with nothing to do, Detach and SetSource with nothing held), so those are the game's few instructions of assembly, alone or in front of a body; elsewhere ours answers what the game's leaves (AL over the last call's answer, the argument's last word, a pointer with AL 1). The context passes several of them on as its own answers.

**The buffer with a drawing context** ([raster_buffer.cpp](../src/core/raster_buffer.cpp), `renderer.raster.buffer`, off by default, no compare mode): the 0x4C-byte buffer panels draw into (vtable 0x00AC1550, a cIGZBuffer of 40 slots; constructor 0x0082B780, destructor 0x0082B7D0), made by the factory's MakeContextBuffer (0x0082B980) together with a drawing context. It has a counted base at +0x04 (vtable 0x00AC1538: AddRef and Release, whose last Release reaches the destructor through 0x0082AE90) and a side interface at +0x0C (vtable 0x00AC1510, interface 0xAB300B2B). It keeps the drawing context (+0x10) and a plain buffer with the pixels (+0x14, vtable 0x00AC1400 in the game), that buffer's rectangle (+0x18) and format (+0x2C), a lock count (+0x28), the context's six surface words (+0x30) and two flags (+0x48 colour key set, +0x49 batch to give back). All 34 of its own functions are ours, seven of them ones that never ran in the game's runs (slots 18, 19, 21, 22, 25 and 28 and the side's 8, below):

- The side's SetContext (slot 7) holds the context and the plain buffer and copies the plain buffer's rectangle, format, bytes a pixel and size; its last call is the context's SetTarget(0), reached with EAX the width, which is SetContext's answer when the context has no target. GetContext (4) points a context without a target at the plain buffer and begins a batch; LockContext and UnlockContext (5, 6) are Lock and Unlock with 0x8010; UseColourOf (9) sets the context's fore colour from a source with the colour interface 0x86D72B57 (its slot 4: red, green, blue and alpha bytes), else to 0xFFFFFFFF.
- Lock (6): the first lock points the context at the plain buffer and ends a batch the context is in, remembering to give it back; every lock reads the context's surface (its slot 46) and counts. Unlock (7) at 0 gives the batch back (BeginBatch); otherwise it leaves AL 1 over the caller's EAX. IsLocked (8).
- Sizes and formats: width and height from the rectangle (its own slot 12), the rectangle copied (11), the format with its depth (14, 15), bits a pixel (16), a colour's pixel value for formats 4 to 9 (30) and a pixel's colour (31, through the helper 0x0082ABF0: 1-5-5-5, 5-6-5 and 4-4-4-4 widened with the low bits copied from the high, alpha 0xFF, 32-bit as it is).
- FillRect (20) and Blt (29) go through the context: push the transform, the clip if one is given, the colour (FillRect, its pixel value made 0xAARRGGBB) or UseColourOf (Blt), the context's FillRect or Blt (Blt's rectangle is the target's moved to the source's corner), pop, and the context's slot 40, whose answer under AL 1 is theirs.
- The colour key (23, 26, 27, with the +0x48 flag) and the hardware cache (37, 38) are jumps to the plain buffer's own slots, so they are those few instructions of assembly (the plain buffer's answers can be the EAX they were reached with), as are Unlock's test and UnlockContext.
- The ones that never ran: FillClip (18: the context's clip filled with a pixel value, its slot 32, between the same push, clip, colour, pop and slot 40 as FillRect) and FillSides (19: FillRect, its own slot 20, by the rectangle's four sides; 0x0082B2F0 is slot 19 of the plain buffer's table 0x00AC1400 and of 0x00ADB464's too, so with the group on theirs is ours); GetPixel (21: the pixel at x, y of the context's surface, slot 46, for 1 to 4 bytes a pixel, else 0) and SetPixel (22: the colour, the context's slot 33 at x, y, and slot 40's whole answer); IsKeyAt (25: the point moved by the context's origin, slot 14, inside the surface's width and height and the plain buffer with a colour key, slot 26, then its own GetPixel there against the key under the format's mask, the helper 0x00825C90 with the format in EAX, which ours inlines: 1 or 0); DrawLine (28: the colour, the pen width the unsigned width as a float, then at most 1 wide the context's slot 34 with the four ends, wider the ends as floats with half of one less than the width added across the line, to the ys when it is at least as wide as it is tall and else to the xs, through Draw, kind 1 and 2 points; AL 1 over slot 40); and the side's Retarget (8: the plain buffer's rectangle again, the context's SetTarget with nothing and then the plain buffer, and a tail jump to its BeginBatch).

Left the game's: the slots shared with other classes (1 to 5, 12, 13, 17, 24, 32 to 36, 39) and the side's 0 and 3, which never ran. The factory around it (vtable 0x00AC1668) is ours too, below.

**The factory** ([raster_factory.cpp](../src/core/raster_factory.cpp), `renderer.raster.factory`, off by default, no compare mode): the system service of class 0xEB2EA3CB (priority 1500000) that a drawing context asks for interface 0x4B2EA3B3 in SetTarget. 0x34 bytes, constructor 0x0082BBF0: the system service base at +0x04 (vtable 0x00AC1694), a third table at +0x08 (0x00AC1650) in place of the base's second, the rasterizer makers (+0x20, a std::list, held), the tracked objects (+0x24, a std::list, not held), the tick count (+0x28) and an RZCriticalSection (+0x2C). Its own table (0x00AC1668) has 11 slots. All 18 of its own functions, its maker's QueryInterface and the buffer class's maker are ours; AddMaker, ReleaseAll, the destructor and the third part's two thunks never ran in the game's runs:

- The constructor and the destructor (0x0082BE00: the tables back, the lock ended, the tracked list destroyed, the makers let go of and their sentinel freed, the service base's destructor, and with flag 1 the memory freed); QueryInterface (slot 0: itself for 0x4B2EA3B3, any other to the base service's by a jump, with EAX the interface, so that part is assembly) and the thunks to it of the service part (0x0082BC60) and the third part (0x0082BC70), and the third part's thunk to the destructor (0x0082BC80), all a few instructions of assembly.
- AddMaker (3): the maker to the makers' front, held, without the lock; it answers the maker's last Release, or with no maker the node before the front (the sentinel).
- MakeRasterizer (4): each maker's slot 3 until one makes a rasterizer; with no makers it answers AL 0 over the caller's EAX (assembly in front of the body). Init puts the only maker there: a 0xC-byte counted object (vtable 0x00AC162C) whose slot 3 is GZRasterizer::Make.
- MakeContextBuffer (5, ret 0x10): its arguments are the out pointer, the width, the height and the colour type (the decompile had them shifted). A plain buffer from the graphics system (SC4::Singleton913C1A, slot 3), set up by its slot 3 with the size and the colour type by value (the kind and its depth); then the buffer with a drawing context and a drawing context, each held, the context's target the plain buffer and the buffer given both; the buffer to the caller, held.
- Track, Untrack and ReleaseIdle (6 to 8), under the lock: the tracked list's front, the first node taken out, and every tracked object that holds something (its slot 9) and was last used (slot 11) five or more ticks ago lets go of it (slot 6). ReleaseAll (9) has every tracked object let go of what it holds, idle or not. Each answers the lock's Unlock (its count).
- The service part's Init (slot 8: the maker, then the framework's AddToTick), Shutdown (9: RemoveFromTick) and OnTick (10: ReleaseIdle(5) when the count divides by 5, then the count goes up; otherwise AL 1 over the count divided by 5, the game's division). Shutdown and OnTick were two places Ghidra's analysis had not reached.
- The maker's QueryInterface (vtable 0x00AC162C, slot 0, 0x0082B850: itself for 1 and 0x6B2EA76C, else AL 0 over the interface less 0x6B2EA76C; its slot 3, GZRasterizer::Make, is in `renderer.raster.rasterizer`) and the buffer with a context's maker for the framework's DLL director (0x0082B820, added by 0x00825B60: a buffer from its constructor, not held, answered as its side interface).

Left the game's: AddRef and Release and slot 10 (the tick count), shared with other classes, and the base service's own slots.

The factory is driven the same way (stages factory-*): factories of the game's constructor over memory filled with a pattern; QueryInterface for its own interface, the base's and none, from both parts; MakeRasterizer with no makers and with recording makers put in front of one another by the game's AddMaker, the one that makes first, last or not there; tracked objects in and out, twice over and not there, ReleaseIdle at tick counts near 0 and 2^32 with last uses on both sides of five ticks, and OnTick; the game's destructor letting the makers go. Then MakeContextBuffer at five sizes in three colour types through the real graphics system, each buffer locked, filled through its FillRect (a game context and a real rasterizer) and its pixels hashed, then released. Last a second factory's Init (the framework is not given it: the real factory has the same ID), a real rasterizer from the maker Init put there for a recording target and a refusal, OnTick and Shutdown; that factory is kept, held once more, since the framework may hold it until its tick ends.

What never ran is driven by address the same way, after the rest (stages buffer-retarget to buffer-rest-life, then factory-addmaker to factory-new-buffer): on a buffer of the game's constructor over a recording context (it records the origin, the pen, the point, the line and the draw too, and its surface is a real 24 by 10 picture) and plain buffers whose colour key answers whether there is one: Retarget with the rectangle changed under it, with and without a target, and on a second plain buffer; FillClip, FillSides and SetPixel in seven formats with and without clips; DrawLine at 14 widths from 0 to 2^32 less one (past 2^24 and 2^31) with ends near and far (the sizes' wrap) and as wide as tall; GetPixel at 0 to 5 bytes a pixel with the surface refused once a round; IsKeyAt in formats 2 to 10 with origins near and far, points inside and outside and keys that are the pixel there with bits inside or outside the mask changed. On factories of the game's constructor: AddMaker with none, three and one twice (MakeRasterizer shows the order); ReleaseAll with nothing tracked and four objects holding or not; the third part's QueryInterface; the destructor through the third part without freeing, then by address with flag 1 on a factory holding makers and a tracked object; the maker as Init makes it asked for seven interfaces; three buffers from 0x0082B820, their constructor's fields hashed, let go by the destructor.

**Checking it.** No compare mode, because the results are calls on the rasterizer, which would run twice. `OPENSC4_TEST_RASTER` also drives the 44 by address on a context made by the game's constructor (over memory filled with a pattern, so the settings' padding is the same in every run) whose rasterizer and target are recording mocks, and whose vtable is a copy of the game's (the draws included; the rasterizer's mock answers how to draw a shape and records the spans): every call on a mock (its slot, its arguments, the points and rectangles behind pointers) and the context's whole state after each call (everything but its pointers, the two stacks by their records) go into the hash, and so do the answers. Inputs: pen widths at the rounding's edges, NaN, infinity and above 2^63; every setter twice and without a rasterizer; origins past 2^24 and near 2^31; clips inside, across, outside and inverted, with the source whole, partial and refused; both stacks past their first allocation and popped past empty; Blt and TiledBlt with empty, inverted and wrapping rectangles; the batch count past 0; three references released; and the shapes through Draw and DrawShape (rectangles and lines inside, across and outside clips of several sizes, ends on pixel centres and halves, every octant and both directions, NaN and huge points, odd counts, the rasterizer answering 0, 2 and 7, the span vector grown); SetTarget with the target it holds and with none, with and without a rasterizer. Breaking the line's end rule on purpose changes the shapes stage's hash.

The buffer with a drawing context is driven the same way (stages buffer-*): on buffers of the game's constructor over a recording context and plain buffer, whose SetTarget and colour-key and cache slots pass on the EAX they were reached with, every method with a known EAX: SetContext new, the same, replaced; QueryInterface for each interface and none; the lock with and without a target and a batch, nested, with flags whose bit 15 and above vary; every format 0 to 10 through the sizes, formats, ToArgb, ColourOf and PixelOf; FillRect and Blt with and without clips from sources with and without a colour; the destructor, and Release through the counted base. Then a buffer of the game's with a context of the game's over a 48 by 40 picture (a real rasterizer from the framework when it locks), filling and blitting through the buffer, unlocking and locking again.

The rasterizer's methods are driven by address too, on a rasterizer of the game's constructor (over memory filled with a pattern) whose target and source are recording cIGZBuffer mocks and whose span renderer is a recording mock put in place of the one Attach made. Each call is made with a known EAX through a few lines of assembly, so the answers that are the caller's EAX are checked as well; every answer (pointers as whether they are the one expected), every call on the mocks with what it was handed, and the rasterizer's whole state after each call (its pointers as which mock they are) go into the hash. Inputs: Attach refused by the query, the lock and the format, then every format; Detach holding both and neither; clips, colours from each first index, modes and flags over whole words; gradients with NaN, infinity and values past 2^31; SetSource with none, refused, at scale 1 and at scales that do not divide 65536, positions on halves and near the rounding's edges, and parts; HowToDraw for every kind short of the polygons; DrawSpans filling with and without a gradient and blending, and copying from every format, keyed or not, refused by the lock, whole, a part and scaled; Release to 0; Make refused and made. Then a drawing on real pixels: a context of the game's constructor with its own vtable, given a target buffer over a 64 by 48 picture through SetTarget, which makes a real rasterizer through the framework (the path the mocks above cannot reach), draws rectangles, frames, Blt and TiledBlt from a 13 by 9 picture, batches and GetSurface, then a second target and none; the pixels and every call on the two buffers go into the hash. Planting a mistake in the lock's assembly (EAX cleared) or in SetSource's rounding (the "one less" dropped) changes the setters' and the source's hashes.

| Check (2026-10-26, release build) | Result |
|---|---|

| The same with SetTarget's stage (2026-10-27), the game's vs all three groups ours | the first seven unchanged, then target 38367A44, life DFE3D9D1 |
| With the rasterizer's stages and GetSurface's two arguments (2026-10-27), the game's vs all five groups ours, and vs `renderer.raster.rasterizer` alone | the same hashes in all 24 stages; context-batch C22BDE0C, rasterizer-made 56B37B33, attach 6AD3F4BB, setters 17E9B88D, gradient D475F126, source 882514AB, how C3C53B42, spans CABB9276, life D1938FC0, draw-target 3DE27919, draw-pixels 56129395, draw-life C3291A58 |

## D3D11 driver

`renderer.d3d11` ([src/core/gd](../src/core/gd)) is OpenSC4's own driver. The driver ([driver.cpp](../src/core/gd/driver.cpp)) keeps the game's state the way the DirectX driver does and calls a backend interface ([backend.h](../src/core/gd/backend.h)); the D3D11 backend ([d3d11_backend.cpp](../src/core/gd/d3d11_backend.cpp), shaders in [d3d11_shaders.h](../src/core/gd/d3d11_shaders.h)) is the only one so far, and a Vulkan one can sit beside it.

**It takes over the OpenGL driver's class ID (0xC4554841), like scvk and SCGL, and is the game's renderer by default** (Dan, 2026-10-22; players who want the original renderer play the original game). The stock DirectX 7 driver stays for developers, for parity runs and render bugs: `renderer.d3d11 = original` turns ours off, and `-d:DirectX` picks the stock driver for one run. The test scripts ([run-autotest.sh](../scripts/run-autotest.sh) and everything built on it: shots, parity, save tests) run the stock driver unless `OPENSC4_REPLACE` or the test folder's `opensc4.ini` names `renderer.d3d11`, so baselines and earlier results stay comparable. The driver is selected whenever it is on: a detour on the driver choice (`cSC4App::SelectGraphicsDriver`, 0x0044CE40) requests it when no `-d:` option names a driver, then restores the saved choice the game would otherwise overwrite, so turning the driver off leaves the user's settings as they were. `-d:DirectX` still picks the stock driver. The first version was made current at PreAppInit with `SetGDriver`, like the pass-through driver, and drew the UI correctly but no terrain or model textures: the game creates 3D textures through a driver pointer it cached before PreAppInit, so they went to the stock DirectX driver and the handles it then bound on ours were unknown. Created by the graphics system at startup, the driver gets everything. It lists its video modes as the DirectX driver does (see "Resolutions"). Full-screen modes change the display mode as the OpenGL driver does (0x0087FC90: width and height only, `CDS_FULLSCREEN`, the desktop's settings back when the mode goes) and use a popup window; a mode the size of the desktop leaves the display alone, which makes it borderless full screen. Switching away from the game gives the desktop its resolution back until the game is active again. Showing a smaller mode scaled on an unchanged desktop is left for later: the game calls `GetCursorPos`/`ScreenToClient` and `SetCursorPos`/`ClientToScreen` itself, so mouse coordinates would need translating in those places too. Tested unattended: 1024x768 full screen on a 1920x1080 desktop matches the windowed run in all 20 shots, and 1920x1080 full screen renders (the game needs `-CustomResolution:enabled` for that size, with either driver; `OPENSC4_GAME_WINDOW="-f -CustomResolution:enabled -r1920x1080x32"` replaces the scripts' default `-w`).

- The back buffer is a texture of our own that keeps its contents; `Flush` copies it to a front-buffer texture, which the window shows as described under "Frame pacing". Buffer regions and snapshots work on these textures; depth regions and clears go through a small full-screen shader, because Direct3D 11 cannot copy part of a depth buffer.
- Drawing: vertices and indices go through ring buffers (the game's arrays are only valid during the call); fans, quads and quad strips become indexed lists; elements a vertex format lacks come from a stride-0 buffer of defaults. One vertex shader does the transforms, the ambient-only lighting, texture coordinate generation and texture matrices, and per-vertex fog; one pixel shader (and a flat-shaded twin) runs up to 8 texture stages of GL texture environment modes or combiners, the alpha test against the game's byte reference, and fog. Input layouts, samplers, blend, depth-stencil and rasterizer states are cached. Textures are BGRA8, or BC1-3 for DXT data that fills whole blocks; uploads are converted on the CPU ([pixels.cpp](../src/core/gd/pixels.cpp)).
- The dynamic vertex buffers keep CPU copies and draw through the same path.
- `d3d11.dll` and `d3dcompiler_47.dll` are loaded only when the driver starts; `debug_layer = on` in `opensc4.ini`'s `[renderer]` section (or `OPENSC4_RENDERER_DEBUG_LAYER=on`) turns on the debug layer. The log records the first occurrence of each primitive, vertex format, texture format, upload format, sampler and stage setup (up to 64 of each).

Texture stages follow the DirectX driver's Direct3D setup (0x00884320, 0x00884500) rather than GL's: the first stage that is off, lacks a texture or uses the blend env mode (which that driver does not implement) ends the cascade; filters map as it maps them, including its `LinearMipmapNearest`, which a missing `break` turns into point-filtered with linear mipmaps; textures created as RGB5 or RGB8 sample an alpha of 1 whatever the uploads hold; modulate on a texture without alpha keeps the current alpha.

**Status: renders the city like the stock driver.** Against the stock DirectX driver in the screenshot harness (2026-10-06, Making Money Tutorial), all 20 shots pass `--tolerance 32 --max-differing 1.2`, at most 0.6% of pixels off by more than 32, all of them deer, which move in real time. With `RenderCloudsAndFog=1` (which the harness turns off because clouds drift in real time), 18 of 20 pass and the two others (zoom 1) differ where the clouds have moved; before the diffuse-source fix above, our driver drew cloud shadows as opaque dark-blue blobs. The tree shadows long had a dark halo that no rendering change fixed: the game drew them differently for our driver, because the driver reported weaker capabilities than the stock one (see "What the game asks the driver" below). `PolygonOffset` maps a z-bias of `n` to a depth bias of `-n` units plus a slope-scaled bias of `-n`, as Wine's OpenGL backend maps `ZBIAS` (`glPolygonOffset(-n, -n)`); unverified, because the test city never sets one. Fog follows Direct3D 7 vertex fog (camera-space depth) and is logged on first use; also unverified, for the same reason. Not done yet: `TexImage2D` (not called). Menus, the region view and UI interaction have not been tried on it yet ([run-game.sh](../scripts/run-game.sh) plays the game in the test user folder).

### Resolutions

The game picks a mode from its video preferences (saved in `SimCity 4.cfg`) or `-r<width>x<height>x<depth>`, `-w` and `-f`; with the command line, sizes outside its own list need `-CustomResolution:enabled` (with either driver, or it quits at startup), which is how players get widescreen sizes.

**Mode list.** The DirectX driver lists the desktop's resolution first, then every other resolution the display offers, each as a full-screen and a windowed mode, all at 32 bits and then all again at 16 bits (144 modes on the AW3423DW; seen with `diagnostics.d3d7_probe`). The D3D11 driver lists them the same way (96 here), so the game picks the same mode indices (11 for 1024x768 windowed, 58 for 1024x768 16-bit full screen). It must: when no mode matches the preferences the game crashes before setting one (0x0098E9A6, an object at + 0xE4 missing). The first version listed only 32-bit modes and, like the OpenGL driver, windowed modes smaller than the desktop, and crashed with a saved 16-bit full-screen preference, which the graphics rules recommend on cards they do not recognise. The driver renders in 32 bits whatever the mode says; in full screen it changes only the display's size (Windows offers no 16-bit desktop modes).

**Settings.** `opensc4.ini` `[renderer]` `resolution` (`WxH` or `desktop`) and `window` (`windowed`, `fullscreen` or `borderless`) override the game's video preferences in memory just before it picks its driver and mode (in the `cSC4App::SelectGraphicsDriver` detour), as sc4-graphics-options does to the saved preferences; colour depth becomes 32 bits. `borderless` is full screen at the desktop's size, which needs no display mode change. No `-CustomResolution` is needed this way, and the game does not write the overridden values to `SimCity 4.cfg` (checked: the next start sees its own preferences again). Unset, the game's own options apply.

**The >2048 px crash is in the stock DirectX driver, not the game.** At 3440x1440 full screen (desktop size, 2026-10-06, Alienware AW3423DW) the stock driver crashes at 0x00884FE1 in its `Disable`: the per-stage state array at driver + 0x11C is null, so its device setup failed silently and the first state change writes through a null pointer. The D3D11 driver runs the same city at 3440x1440 with no problem (20 screenshots and a full test run), so with it the cap is gone.

A windowed size larger than the desktop crashes in game code instead (0x0098E9A6, an object at + 0xE4 missing; tried 2000x1000 and 2560x1440 on a 1920x1080 desktop), which matters only for testing: the window cannot be bigger than the screen anyway.

### Frame pacing

`Flush` is not a frame boundary. The framework's main loop (0x008795BD) never waits while the game runs: it handles window messages, then calls the framework's idle step (0x0087A812), which ticks every tick service (the simulation among them) and every fourth pass the on-idle services, among them the window manager's update (0x0098E5C0), which draws the software cursor and calls `Flush` when the view changed. How often that happens depends on what the loop is doing (2026-10-06, D3D11 driver, logged every 10 s):

| Phase | Flushes per second | Frames shown per second (175 Hz display) |
|---|---|---|
| Start-up and loading screens | about 20,000 | 172-174 |
| City running at speed 2 | about 15 | about 20-30 (front-buffer updates count too) |

So while the simulation runs, the game itself redraws the city about 15 times a second; the stock driver's total flush count over the same run is similar. Changing the on-idle interval (`cIGZFrameWork::SetOnIdleInterval`, default 4) to 1 or 16 made no difference to either rate or to the simulation.

Waiting for vertical sync in `Flush` starves that loop: in a 60 s run at speed 2, a blocking `Present(1)` showed 7.5 frames per second and the city reached 2010-01-25, where the stock driver reached 2011-12-09. So the driver paces what the window shows, not the game: a flip-model swap chain with a frame-latency waitable object (maximum latency 1). `Flush` copies the back buffer to the front buffer and posts the driver's window a message; the front buffer is presented when the game's main loop dispatches it, between passes, if the waitable object is signalled (else a 10 ms window timer retries). **Presenting inside `Flush` showed stale pictures** (seen by Dan on 2026-10-06, the first time anyone watched the driver live): some flushes come in the middle of a redraw, after the 3D view's saved image is restored and before the changes are drawn, and the first version presented the first `Flush` after the display was ready, which kept such an image on screen for a whole refresh. Grabbing the screen 40 times in a row (20 ms apart) on a static view showed earlier images coming back (`E F E G ... E`); with presentation between loop passes none do. The stock drivers show every `Flush` at once, so the intermediate images last only microseconds there. The CPU is kept at most 3 flushes ahead of the GPU with event queries, since nothing else blocks (the GPU is not the limit: about 20 us of waiting per flush). Same 60 s run: the city reaches 2011-12-16, as with the stock driver. `vsync = off` in `opensc4.ini`'s `[renderer]` section (or `OPENSC4_RENDERER_VSYNC=off`) presents every `Flush` at once (tearing allowed where supported); without flip-model support (before Windows 8.1) the driver falls back to a blt-model swap chain presenting every `Flush`.

**The game caps its frame rate by simulation speed** (what sc4-disable-fps-limits patches): `cSC4Simulator`'s speed setter (0x00702430, from `SetSimSpeed` and `Init`) stores 30, 20 or 15 frames per second for turtle, rhino and cheetah in the simulator at + 0x84 (the interface pointer's offset; a cheat, message 0x0AC07E24, also sets it). The only reader (0x00703EDD, found with a hardware read watchpoint; 0x00703E70 reads it as + 0x7C from a base 8 bytes further on) turns it into the simulation's time slice per frame: 1000 / cap ms, or 1000 / 30 under a condition at + 0x28 / + 0x2C, minus the time a timer at + 0x58 measured, at least 15 ms, stored at + 0x70. So the cap splits each loop between simulating and drawing. Measured in the test city at cheetah for 60 s (D3D11 driver, the field set after `SetSimSpeed`):

| Cap | Redraws per second | Simulation reached |
|---|---|---|
| 15 (the game's) | 15 | 2011-12-08 |
| 30 | 29 | 2011-12-01 |
| 60 | 57 | 2011-11-29 |
| 255 (sc4-disable-fps-limits) | 57 | 2011-12-02 |

Above about 57 the 15 ms minimum slice is the limit. In this small city the simulation does not use its whole slice, so four times the redraws cost almost nothing; a large city would lose simulation speed (not measured: the test region has only the tutorial cities).

Not changed: the loop never sleeps, so the game keeps a CPU core busy even when paused, as with the stock driver. Lowering that means changing the framework loop (for example waiting when paused), not the driver. Smoother motion while the simulation runs would need the simulation and drawing taken apart, which is far beyond the renderer.

### What the game asks the driver

The game configures itself from `Graphics Rules.sgr` (in the install folder), evaluated against values it collects at startup (0x007CF780): `textureStages`, `DXT`, the driver and card strings, memory, CPU speed and so on. The rules then decide, among other things, whether shadows use a second texture stage (`useSecondStage`, rule "Two stages": at least 2 stages), texture depth (`texturebits`, 16 under "No DXT"), the recommended shadow, effects and traffic levels ("Fast card": card names such as `NVidia*Geforce*`) and dirty-rectangle merging. Two driver calls feed it:

- **`GetVideoModeInfo` for the current mode.** Beyond the size and masks, `sGDMode` carries capabilities (field names from gzcom-dll's `sGDMode.h`): stencil (0x21), texture stage count (0x24), multitexture, texture env combine, NV combine4 and fog coordinate support (0x28-0x2B), DXT support (0x2C) and an initialised flag (0x30). The DirectX driver fills them only in the current mode; on this machine it reports 8 stages, multitexture, combine, fog coordinates, DXT and stencil, no combine4, an alpha mask of 0 for 32-bit modes, and 1 in the unknown bytes 0x22 and 0x2F.
- **`GetDriverInfo`.** Newline-separated: `Maxis 3D GDriver`, then sglDriverName, sglDriverVersion, driverName, driverVersion, cardName and cardVersion (the DirectX driver: `DirectX`, `2.0`, the display driver DLL, its version and device GUID, the adapter name, `Vendor: 10de, Device: 2208, Board: 153510de, Chipset: 00a1`).

Our driver first reported 1 texture stage, no DXT and empty strings (the mode fields had been copied from the OpenGL driver's list without being decoded). The game then left out the second shadow stage and drew shadows on models in a separate pass, which produced the halo, chose 16-bit textures and lower recommended settings. It now reports what the DirectX driver reports, with the real adapter from DXGI and `DirectX` as sglDriverName (what the rules were tuned on), and only the driver name and version lines name OpenSC4. **To check:** run the game with `-writeLog:on` (for example `OPENSC4_GAME_ARGS=-writeLog:on`): it writes `<computer name>-config-log.txt` next to the exe with every collected value and every rule applied. With either driver the logs now differ only in those two lines.

`diagnostics.d3d7_probe` ([d3d7_probe.cpp](../src/core/d3d7_probe.cpp)), used with the stock driver, logs the Direct3D 7 state the DirectX driver actually sets on its draws (render states, texture stage states, material, texture surface format), its `GetDriverInfo` and its mode records: the ground truth when our driver and the stock one disagree.
